People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.

Church is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like I’ll be trying to do something, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or even just a thought pops in about another job I’m supposed to get to, and I’m gone. I lose the thread completely.\n\nIt’s like the whole day gets away from me before I even realize it. I mean, I’ve worked around noise and people my whole life, being in carpentry and all, so it’s not like I’m not used to it—but lately it just pulls me right off whatever I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m stuck at the desk doing estimates, or sitting through one of those long meetings, I get this wound-up feeling inside, like I need to stand up or walk around or go check something in the shop.\n\nIt’s funny, because my wrist’s healed now, so it’s not like pain is making me move or anything. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish the paperwork, but my body feels like it’s ready to go do something else. Even if I stay in the chair, inside I’m just… revving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll jump into a task because it’s right in front of me, but… acting without thinking, like doing reckless things? I don’t know.\n\nIt’s funny, in carpentry you kind of learn not to be too impulsive, because if you cut once without measuring—well, my old foreman used to say you’re just making expensive firewood. He was a character, that guy. Always had a pencil behind his ear and somehow never used it.\n\nBut, sorry—what I mean is, I haven’t really noticed myself doing things on impulse like that. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks or projects because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I’ll start something—like an estimate, or even just trying to finish one piece of a job—and then one saw starts up, or someone walks by the office, or I suddenly remember some other job I’m supposed to check on, and that’s it. I’m off it.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “I’ll just handle this one thing real quick,” but of course it turns into three other things. Like in the shop, you go looking for one clamp and end up noticing the blades need sorting, or someone left something where they shouldn’t have—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yeah, I lose focus so easily now that I don’t finish what I meant to finish. Whole days can get away from me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these difficulties with focus and restlessness are affecting your relationships with others or your overall work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays have started costing me. I’ve lost commissions because I didn’t get estimates done in time, or I missed something and then the job needed rework. And the crew—well, they’re polite about it, mostly, but I can tell they don’t trust me as much with scheduling or ordering materials anymore. That stings, because I used to be the one people counted on for that.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up a lot of slack. Bills, reminders, keeping track of papers… I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” which is basically the kiss of death in our house. Then we’re tearing through drawers looking for it. We used to have this junk drawer by the phone—well, back when people had phones on the wall—and now every drawer feels like that.\n\nSorry. But yes, it’s affecting both. Work and home.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable about… a little over a year ago. Maybe a year and a month now.\n\nIt was after I broke my wrist and they moved me off the hands-on carpentry for a while. I got shifted into more shop paperwork and supervising, and that’s when it started really showing up. Before that, if I was working with my hands, there was kind of a rhythm to it—you measure, cut, fit, fix the thing in front of you. Paperwork doesn’t have that same… well, it just sits there and dares you to finish it.\n\nSo, yeah. This level of trouble started after the wrist break, when my work changed. About thirteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant feelings of anxiety, depression, or irritability lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anything “significant,” exactly. I mean, I get frustrated—who wouldn’t, when you’re staring at the same paper for half an hour and somehow you’ve sharpened three pencils and moved a stack of catalogs instead of finishing it.\n\nBut anxiety or depression… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that in some big way. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but then again the shop can make anybody irritable. The compressor alone—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing that stands out to me as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily routines or activities at home, beyond just the issues with bills and reminders?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with losing things. I mean, not “lost” like gone forever, usually, but I’m constantly hunting for my keys, my phone, my tape measure… order sheets, too. I’ll have a note in my hand, set it down for one second, and then it’s like it evaporated.\n\nAnd then I lose ten, fifteen minutes looking for it, except it happens over and over, so it eats up half the day. The guys at work have started sort of checking behind me—like, “Did you leave it on the bench?” or “Is it in your apron?” Which is helpful, but also embarrassing. At home it’s the same kind of thing, just with keys and phone and whatever paper I swear I just had.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and losing things are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I hate saying “a lot,” because it sounds dramatic, but… yes, it’s causing real problems.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve made mistakes on paperwork or ordering, and then we’re delayed, or something has to be redone. There’ve been commissions I didn’t get because I was late or messed up the details. And the crew, they’re good people, but I can tell they don’t really want to depend on me for scheduling or ordering now. They’ll double-check me, or just ask someone else.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband’s had to take over the bills and reminders. I used to handle more of that, but I kept misplacing papers or missing what needed to be done. Then I’d find the envelope later under a magazine or in the wrong stack, which is ridiculous because I have all these little piles that are supposed to be “organized.” My mother used to do that with recipes, actually—sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nSo yes. It’s embarrassing, and it’s costing me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it shows up both places, just a little differently.\n\nAt work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away. If I mess up an order or I’m late getting details together, then the job gets delayed, or we have to redo something, and that’s happened enough that the crew doesn’t really lean on me for scheduling or ordering like they used to. I’ve lost commissions over it, which still makes me feel sick to think about.\n\nAt home, it’s quieter, I guess, but it’s still there. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I keep putting papers somewhere “safe” and then can’t find them, or I miss what needs doing. I’ll have a stack by the chair, a stack on the counter—my aunt used to have piles like that with coupons, actually, and she always knew where everything was, somehow. I do not.\n\nSo yes, it’s at home too. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness in the evenings or weekends when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yes, weekends too. Honestly, sometimes it’s worse when everything is supposed to be calm. Like at home in the evening, or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, shifting around, whispering something to my husband when I really don’t need to—he’ll give me that look, you know.\n\nAnd I’ll get up for no real reason, like I suddenly have to check something, but really it’s just… sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable. It’s not like work, where there’s noise and tools and people moving around. Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. I’ll start doing one thing, like picking something up or putting tools away, and then I notice something else, or hear a noise, or remember some other job I meant to check on, and suddenly I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nIt’s like what I was saying—what brings me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. One saw starting up, somebody walking past, even just a thought about another job, and I’m pulled off. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Quiet places are actually kind of hard for me, even places where you’re supposed to just sit and be still.\n\nLike at home, if everything’s quiet, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers or shifting around in the chair. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. I mean, I’ve worked around noise and people my whole life, being in carpentry and all, so it’s not like I’m not used to it—but lately it just pulls me right off whatever I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m stuck at the desk doing estimates, or sitting through one of those long meetings, I get this wound-up feeling inside, like I need to stand up or walk around or go check something in the shop.\n\nIt’s funny, because my wrist’s healed now, so it’s not like pain is making me move or anything. I mean, sometimes I’ll jump into a task because it’s right in front of me, but… acting without thinking, like doing reckless things? I don’t know.\n\nIt’s funny, in carpentry you kind of learn not to be too impulsive, because if you cut once without measuring—well, my old foreman used to say you’re just making expensive firewood. He was a character, that guy. Always had a pencil behind his ear and somehow never used it.\n\nBut, sorry—what I mean is, I haven’t really noticed myself doing things on impulse like that. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks or projects because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I’ll start something—like an estimate, or even just trying to finish one piece of a job—and then one saw starts up, or someone walks by the office, or I suddenly remember some other job I’m supposed to check on, and that’s it. I’m off it.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “I’ll just handle this one thing real quick,” but of course it turns into three other things. Like in the shop, you go looking for one clamp and end up noticing the blades need sorting, or someone left something where they shouldn’t have—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yeah, I lose focus so easily now that I don’t finish what I meant to finish. Whole days can get away from me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these difficulties with focus and restlessness are affecting your relationships with others or your overall work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays have started costing me. I’ve lost commissions because I didn’t get estimates done in time, or I missed something and then the job needed rework. And the crew—well, they’re polite about it, mostly, but I can tell they don’t trust me as much with scheduling or ordering materials anymore. That stings, because I used to be the one people counted on for that.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up a lot of slack. Bills, reminders, keeping track of papers… I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” which is basically the kiss of death in our house. Then we’re tearing through drawers looking for it. We used to have this junk drawer by the phone—well, back when people had phones on the wall—and now every drawer feels like that.\n\nSorry. But yes, it’s affecting both. Work and home.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable about… a little over a year ago. Maybe a year and a month now.\n\nIt was after I broke my wrist and they moved me off the hands-on carpentry for a while. I got shifted into more shop paperwork and supervising, and that’s when it started really showing up. Before that, if I was working with my hands, there was kind of a rhythm to it—you measure, cut, fit, fix the thing in front of you. Paperwork doesn’t have that same… well, it just sits there and dares you to finish it.\n\nSo, yeah. This level of trouble started after the wrist break, when my work changed. About thirteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant feelings of anxiety, depression, or irritability lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anything “significant,” exactly. I mean, I get frustrated—who wouldn’t, when you’re staring at the same paper for half an hour and somehow you’ve sharpened three pencils and moved a stack of catalogs instead of finishing it.\n\nBut anxiety or depression… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that in some big way. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but then again the shop can make anybody irritable. The compressor alone—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing that stands out to me as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily routines or activities at home, beyond just the issues with bills and reminders?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with losing things. I mean, not “lost” like gone forever, usually, but I’m constantly hunting for my keys, my phone, my tape measure… order sheets, too. I’ll have a note in my hand, set it down for one second, and then it’s like it evaporated.\n\nAnd then I lose ten, fifteen minutes looking for it, except it happens over and over, so it eats up half the day. The guys at work have started sort of checking behind me—like, “Did you leave it on the bench?” or “Is it in your apron?” Which is helpful, but also embarrassing. At home it’s the same kind of thing, just with keys and phone and whatever paper I swear I just had.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and losing things are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I hate saying “a lot,” because it sounds dramatic, but… yes, it’s causing real problems.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve made mistakes on paperwork or ordering, and then we’re delayed, or something has to be redone. There’ve been commissions I didn’t get because I was late or messed up the details. And the crew, they’re good people, but I can tell they don’t really want to depend on me for scheduling or ordering now. They’ll double-check me, or just ask someone else.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband’s had to take over the bills and reminders. I used to handle more of that, but I kept misplacing papers or missing what needed to be done. Then I’d find the envelope later under a magazine or in the wrong stack, which is ridiculous because I have all these little piles that are supposed to be “organized.” My mother used to do that with recipes, actually—sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nSo yes. It’s embarrassing, and it’s costing me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it shows up both places, just a little differently.\n\nAt work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away. If I mess up an order or I’m late getting details together, then the job gets delayed, or we have to redo something, and that’s happened enough that the crew doesn’t really lean on me for scheduling or ordering like they used to. I’ve lost commissions over it, which still makes me feel sick to think about.\n\nAt home, it’s quieter, I guess, but it’s still there. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I keep putting papers somewhere “safe” and then can’t find them, or I miss what needs doing. I’ll have a stack by the chair, a stack on the counter—my aunt used to have piles like that with coupons, actually, and she always knew where everything was, somehow. I do not.\n\nSo yes, it’s at home too. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness in the evenings or weekends when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yes, weekends too. Honestly, sometimes it’s worse when everything is supposed to be calm. Like at home in the evening, or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, shifting around, whispering something to my husband when I really don’t need to—he’ll give me that look, you know.\n\nAnd I’ll get up for no real reason, like I suddenly have to check something, but really it’s just… sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable. It’s not like work, where there’s noise and tools and people moving around. Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. I’ll start doing one thing, like picking something up or putting tools away, and then I notice something else, or hear a noise, or remember some other job I meant to check on, and suddenly I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nIt’s like what I was saying—what brings me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. One saw starting up, somebody walking past, even just a thought about another job, and I’m pulled off. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Quiet places are actually kind of hard for me, even places where you’re supposed to just sit and be still.\n\nLike at home, if everything’s quiet, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers or shifting around in the chair. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen in about the same way at work, at home, and when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.\n\nAt work, it’s not just me being scattered—my mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions. I’ve had to redo work on jobs because I missed something or didn’t keep track of what needed ordering. And now I can tell the crew doesn’t want to lean on me as much for scheduling or materials, which… that’s hard, because I’ve been doing this a long time.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just in a different way. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be handled. I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” and then of course I can’t find it. Then I’m tearing through drawers, and that turns into reorganizing a whole cabinet, and—sorry, I know I’m wandering. But yes, it’s pretty much across the board.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen in about the same way at work, at home, and when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.\n\nAt work, it’s not just me being scattered—my mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions. I’ve had to redo work on jobs because I missed something or didn’t keep track of what needed ordering. And now I can tell the crew doesn’t want to lean on me as much for scheduling or materials, which… that’s hard, because I’ve been doing this a long time.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just in a different way. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be handled. I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” and then of course I can’t find it. Then I’m tearing through drawers, and that turns into reorganizing a whole cabinet, and—sorry, I know I’m wandering. But yes, it’s pretty much across the board.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're just as easily distracted and restless when you're doing something you really enjoy, like a hobby, as when you're doing everyday tasks or chores?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / carpenter / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is I cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore—one saw starting up, somebody walking past the office, or even a thought about another job will pull me off what I’m doing, and then the whole day gets away from me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… even when it’s something I like, I still get pulled off track. It’s not really about whether I enjoy it or not.

What brings me in is that I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I’ll be trying to focus, and one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just a thought about another job I need to check on will pull me away. And then I’m off doing that, or thinking about that, and the thing I meant to be doing is just… gone.

And then somehow the whole day gets away from me. I’ll look up and realize I didn’t finish what I started, even if I actually wanted to do it.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like I’ll be trying to do something, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or even just a thought pops in about another job I’m supposed to get to, and I’m gone. I lose the thread completely.\n\nIt’s like the whole day gets away from me before I even realize it. I mean, I’ve worked around noise and people my whole life, being in carpentry and all, so it’s not like I’m not used to it—but lately it just pulls me right off whatever I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m stuck at the desk doing estimates, or sitting through one of those long meetings, I get this wound-up feeling inside, like I need to stand up or walk around or go check something in the shop.\n\nIt’s funny, because my wrist’s healed now, so it’s not like pain is making me move or anything. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish the paperwork, but my body feels like it’s ready to go do something else. Even if I stay in the chair, inside I’m just… revving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll jump into a task because it’s right in front of me, but… acting without thinking, like doing reckless things? I don’t know.\n\nIt’s funny, in carpentry you kind of learn not to be too impulsive, because if you cut once without measuring—well, my old foreman used to say you’re just making expensive firewood. He was a character, that guy. Always had a pencil behind his ear and somehow never used it.\n\nBut, sorry—what I mean is, I haven’t really noticed myself doing things on impulse like that. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks or projects because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I’ll start something—like an estimate, or even just trying to finish one piece of a job—and then one saw starts up, or someone walks by the office, or I suddenly remember some other job I’m supposed to check on, and that’s it. I’m off it.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “I’ll just handle this one thing real quick,” but of course it turns into three other things. Like in the shop, you go looking for one clamp and end up noticing the blades need sorting, or someone left something where they shouldn’t have—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yeah, I lose focus so easily now that I don’t finish what I meant to finish. Whole days can get away from me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these difficulties with focus and restlessness are affecting your relationships with others or your overall work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays have started costing me. I’ve lost commissions because I didn’t get estimates done in time, or I missed something and then the job needed rework. And the crew—well, they’re polite about it, mostly, but I can tell they don’t trust me as much with scheduling or ordering materials anymore. That stings, because I used to be the one people counted on for that.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up a lot of slack. Bills, reminders, keeping track of papers… I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” which is basically the kiss of death in our house. Then we’re tearing through drawers looking for it. We used to have this junk drawer by the phone—well, back when people had phones on the wall—and now every drawer feels like that.\n\nSorry. But yes, it’s affecting both. Work and home.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable about… a little over a year ago. Maybe a year and a month now.\n\nIt was after I broke my wrist and they moved me off the hands-on carpentry for a while. I got shifted into more shop paperwork and supervising, and that’s when it started really showing up. Before that, if I was working with my hands, there was kind of a rhythm to it—you measure, cut, fit, fix the thing in front of you. Paperwork doesn’t have that same… well, it just sits there and dares you to finish it.\n\nSo, yeah. This level of trouble started after the wrist break, when my work changed. About thirteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant feelings of anxiety, depression, or irritability lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anything “significant,” exactly. I mean, I get frustrated—who wouldn’t, when you’re staring at the same paper for half an hour and somehow you’ve sharpened three pencils and moved a stack of catalogs instead of finishing it.\n\nBut anxiety or depression… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that in some big way. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but then again the shop can make anybody irritable. The compressor alone—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing that stands out to me as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily routines or activities at home, beyond just the issues with bills and reminders?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with losing things. I mean, not “lost” like gone forever, usually, but I’m constantly hunting for my keys, my phone, my tape measure… order sheets, too. I’ll have a note in my hand, set it down for one second, and then it’s like it evaporated.\n\nAnd then I lose ten, fifteen minutes looking for it, except it happens over and over, so it eats up half the day. The guys at work have started sort of checking behind me—like, “Did you leave it on the bench?” or “Is it in your apron?” Which is helpful, but also embarrassing. At home it’s the same kind of thing, just with keys and phone and whatever paper I swear I just had.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and losing things are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I hate saying “a lot,” because it sounds dramatic, but… yes, it’s causing real problems.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve made mistakes on paperwork or ordering, and then we’re delayed, or something has to be redone. There’ve been commissions I didn’t get because I was late or messed up the details. And the crew, they’re good people, but I can tell they don’t really want to depend on me for scheduling or ordering now. They’ll double-check me, or just ask someone else.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband’s had to take over the bills and reminders. I used to handle more of that, but I kept misplacing papers or missing what needed to be done. Then I’d find the envelope later under a magazine or in the wrong stack, which is ridiculous because I have all these little piles that are supposed to be “organized.” My mother used to do that with recipes, actually—sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nSo yes. It’s embarrassing, and it’s costing me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it shows up both places, just a little differently.\n\nAt work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away. If I mess up an order or I’m late getting details together, then the job gets delayed, or we have to redo something, and that’s happened enough that the crew doesn’t really lean on me for scheduling or ordering like they used to. I’ve lost commissions over it, which still makes me feel sick to think about.\n\nAt home, it’s quieter, I guess, but it’s still there. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I keep putting papers somewhere “safe” and then can’t find them, or I miss what needs doing. I’ll have a stack by the chair, a stack on the counter—my aunt used to have piles like that with coupons, actually, and she always knew where everything was, somehow. I do not.\n\nSo yes, it’s at home too. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness in the evenings or weekends when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yes, weekends too. Honestly, sometimes it’s worse when everything is supposed to be calm. Like at home in the evening, or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, shifting around, whispering something to my husband when I really don’t need to—he’ll give me that look, you know.\n\nAnd I’ll get up for no real reason, like I suddenly have to check something, but really it’s just… sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable. It’s not like work, where there’s noise and tools and people moving around. Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. I’ll start doing one thing, like picking something up or putting tools away, and then I notice something else, or hear a noise, or remember some other job I meant to check on, and suddenly I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nIt’s like what I was saying—what brings me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. One saw starting up, somebody walking past, even just a thought about another job, and I’m pulled off. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Quiet places are actually kind of hard for me, even places where you’re supposed to just sit and be still.\n\nLike at home, if everything’s quiet, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers or shifting around in the chair. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen in about the same way at work, at home, and when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.\n\nAt work, it’s not just me being scattered—my mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions. I’ve had to redo work on jobs because I missed something or didn’t keep track of what needed ordering. And now I can tell the crew doesn’t want to lean on me as much for scheduling or materials, which… that’s hard, because I’ve been doing this a long time.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just in a different way. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be handled. I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” and then of course I can’t find it. If I mess up an order or I’m late getting details together, then the job gets delayed, or we have to redo something, and that’s happened enough that the crew doesn’t really lean on me for scheduling or ordering like they used to. I’ve lost commissions over it, which still makes me feel sick to think about.\n\nAt home, it’s quieter, I guess, but it’s still there. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I keep putting papers somewhere “safe” and then can’t find them, or I miss what needs doing. I’ll have a stack by the chair, a stack on the counter—my aunt used to have piles like that with coupons, actually, and she always knew where everything was, somehow. I do not.\n\nSo yes, it’s at home too. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness in the evenings or weekends when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yes, weekends too. Honestly, sometimes it’s worse when everything is supposed to be calm. Like at home in the evening, or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, shifting around, whispering something to my husband when I really don’t need to—he’ll give me that look, you know.\n\nAnd I’ll get up for no real reason, like I suddenly have to check something, but really it’s just… sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable. It’s not like work, where there’s noise and tools and people moving around. Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. I’ll start doing one thing, like picking something up or putting tools away, and then I notice something else, or hear a noise, or remember some other job I meant to check on, and suddenly I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nIt’s like what I was saying—what brings me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. One saw starting up, somebody walking past, even just a thought about another job, and I’m pulled off. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Quiet places are actually kind of hard for me, even places where you’re supposed to just sit and be still.\n\nLike at home, if everything’s quiet, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers or shifting around in the chair. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen in about the same way at work, at home, and when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.\n\nAt work, it’s not just me being scattered—my mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions. I’ve had to redo work on jobs because I missed something or didn’t keep track of what needed ordering. And now I can tell the crew doesn’t want to lean on me as much for scheduling or materials, which… that’s hard, because I’ve been doing this a long time.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just in a different way. Then I’m tearing through drawers, and that turns into reorganizing a whole cabinet, and—sorry, I know I’m wandering. It’s not really about whether I enjoy it or not.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Yeah, I do. I wish it was only with chores, but it isn’t. Even if I’m interested, I just can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.

Not physically always, but my head is gone. And then I’ll lose the thread of what I was doing, and next thing I know the whole day has gotten away from me.

It’s frustrating, because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just get pulled off so easily.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like I’ll be trying to do something, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or even just a thought pops in about another job I’m supposed to get to, and I’m gone. I lose the thread completely.\n\nIt’s like the whole day gets away from me before I even realize it. I mean, I’ve worked around noise and people my whole life, being in carpentry and all, so it’s not like I’m not used to it—but lately it just pulls me right off whatever I’m doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're trying to sit still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. If I’m stuck at the desk doing estimates, or sitting through one of those long meetings, I get this wound-up feeling inside, like I need to stand up or walk around or go check something in the shop.\n\nIt’s funny, because my wrist’s healed now, so it’s not like pain is making me move or anything. I know I’m supposed to just sit there and finish the paperwork, but my body feels like it’s ready to go do something else. Even if I stay in the chair, inside I’m just… revving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, exactly. I mean, sometimes I’ll jump into a task because it’s right in front of me, but… acting without thinking, like doing reckless things? I don’t know.\n\nIt’s funny, in carpentry you kind of learn not to be too impulsive, because if you cut once without measuring—well, my old foreman used to say you’re just making expensive firewood. He was a character, that guy. Always had a pencil behind his ear and somehow never used it.\n\nBut, sorry—what I mean is, I haven’t really noticed myself doing things on impulse like that. Not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble completing tasks or projects because you lose focus or get distracted?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly it. I’ll start something—like an estimate, or even just trying to finish one piece of a job—and then one saw starts up, or someone walks by the office, or I suddenly remember some other job I’m supposed to check on, and that’s it. I’m off it.\n\nAnd then I’ll think, “I’ll just handle this one thing real quick,” but of course it turns into three other things. Like in the shop, you go looking for one clamp and end up noticing the blades need sorting, or someone left something where they shouldn’t have—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yeah, I lose focus so easily now that I don’t finish what I meant to finish. Whole days can get away from me like that.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding that these difficulties with focus and restlessness are affecting your relationships with others or your overall work performance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. More than I want to admit, honestly.\n\nAt work, the mistakes and delays have started costing me. I’ve lost commissions because I didn’t get estimates done in time, or I missed something and then the job needed rework. And the crew—well, they’re polite about it, mostly, but I can tell they don’t trust me as much with scheduling or ordering materials anymore. That stings, because I used to be the one people counted on for that.\n\nAnd at home, my husband’s had to pick up a lot of slack. Bills, reminders, keeping track of papers… I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” which is basically the kiss of death in our house. Then we’re tearing through drawers looking for it. We used to have this junk drawer by the phone—well, back when people had phones on the wall—and now every drawer feels like that.\n\nSorry. But yes, it’s affecting both. Work and home.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice these difficulties with focus and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’d say it really became noticeable about… a little over a year ago. Maybe a year and a month now.\n\nIt was after I broke my wrist and they moved me off the hands-on carpentry for a while. I got shifted into more shop paperwork and supervising, and that’s when it started really showing up. Before that, if I was working with my hands, there was kind of a rhythm to it—you measure, cut, fit, fix the thing in front of you. Paperwork doesn’t have that same… well, it just sits there and dares you to finish it.\n\nSo, yeah. This level of trouble started after the wrist break, when my work changed. About thirteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant feelings of anxiety, depression, or irritability lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anything “significant,” exactly. I mean, I get frustrated—who wouldn’t, when you’re staring at the same paper for half an hour and somehow you’ve sharpened three pencils and moved a stack of catalogs instead of finishing it.\n\nBut anxiety or depression… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that in some big way. Irritable, maybe sometimes, but then again the shop can make anybody irritable. The compressor alone—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing that stands out to me as the main problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble with daily routines or activities at home, beyond just the issues with bills and reminders?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly with losing things. I mean, not “lost” like gone forever, usually, but I’m constantly hunting for my keys, my phone, my tape measure… order sheets, too. I’ll have a note in my hand, set it down for one second, and then it’s like it evaporated.\n\nAnd then I lose ten, fifteen minutes looking for it, except it happens over and over, so it eats up half the day. The guys at work have started sort of checking behind me—like, “Did you leave it on the bench?” or “Is it in your apron?” Which is helpful, but also embarrassing. At home it’s the same kind of thing, just with keys and phone and whatever paper I swear I just had.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these difficulties with focus, restlessness, and losing things are causing you a lot of distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I hate saying “a lot,” because it sounds dramatic, but… yes, it’s causing real problems.\n\nAt work it’s not just annoying anymore. I’ve made mistakes on paperwork or ordering, and then we’re delayed, or something has to be redone. There’ve been commissions I didn’t get because I was late or messed up the details. And the crew, they’re good people, but I can tell they don’t really want to depend on me for scheduling or ordering now. They’ll double-check me, or just ask someone else.\n\nAnd at home—well, my husband’s had to take over the bills and reminders. I used to handle more of that, but I kept misplacing papers or missing what needed to be done. Then I’d find the envelope later under a magazine or in the wrong stack, which is ridiculous because I have all these little piles that are supposed to be “organized.” My mother used to do that with recipes, actually—sorry, that’s not the point.\n\nSo yes. It’s embarrassing, and it’s costing me, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness at home as much as you do at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, it shows up both places, just a little differently.\n\nAt work it’s more obvious because there are consequences right away. If I mess up an order or I’m late getting details together, then the job gets delayed, or we have to redo something, and that’s happened enough that the crew doesn’t really lean on me for scheduling or ordering like they used to. I’ve lost commissions over it, which still makes me feel sick to think about.\n\nAt home, it’s quieter, I guess, but it’s still there. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I keep putting papers somewhere “safe” and then can’t find them, or I miss what needs doing. I’ll have a stack by the chair, a stack on the counter—my aunt used to have piles like that with coupons, actually, and she always knew where everything was, somehow. I do not.\n\nSo yes, it’s at home too. Work just makes it harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these difficulties with focus and restlessness in the evenings or weekends when you're not working?\n\nPatient: Yes, weekends too. Honestly, sometimes it’s worse when everything is supposed to be calm. Like at home in the evening, or in church, I’ll catch myself tapping my foot, shifting around, whispering something to my husband when I really don’t need to—he’ll give me that look, you know.\n\nAnd I’ll get up for no real reason, like I suddenly have to check something, but really it’s just… sitting still in the quiet feels almost unbearable. It’s not like work, where there’s noise and tools and people moving around. Quiet makes me notice it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your difficulties with focus and restlessness are worse at certain times of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clean pattern like that. I wish I could say, “Oh, it’s always mornings,” or “it’s always evenings,” but it doesn’t feel that organized.\n\nSome days mornings are bad because I’m trying to get going and I’ll start one thing, then remember something else, and then—well, yesterday I found myself looking for a pencil in the laundry room, which makes no sense except I guess I’d been there earlier. But then evenings can be bad too, especially if it’s quiet or I’m tired.\n\nSo… I don’t know that it’s a certain time of day exactly. It just kind of shows up, and the situation matters more than the clock, I think.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing things you enjoy, like hobbies?\n\nPatient: Yes… even with things I like, it happens. I’ll start doing something and then one little thing pulls me away—like if a saw starts up outside, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly remember another job I meant to check on.\n\nAnd then I’m gone from what I was doing. It’s not that I don’t care about it. I do. It’s just like my mind gets yanked sideways, and by the time I come back, the whole day has sort of slipped away from me. That’s really what made me come in—I can’t seem to keep my mind on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus when you're doing things by yourself, compared to when you're with other people?\n\nPatient: Yes, by myself is when it really gets away from me, I think. If there are other people right there, sometimes they kind of keep me anchored, or at least I’m aware I’m supposed to stay on track.\n\nBut alone… what brings me in is I just cannot keep my mind on one thing anymore. I’ll be trying to do paperwork or look over something for a job, and one saw starts up, or somebody walks past the office, or I suddenly think, “Oh, I need to check that other job,” and then I’m off. And then somehow it’s an hour later, or the whole day’s gone, and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not like I’m choosing to wander off. It just happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus when you're doing routine chores or tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, routine stuff at home is probably one of the worst places for it, honestly. I’ll start doing one thing, like picking something up or putting tools away, and then I notice something else, or hear a noise, or remember some other job I meant to check on, and suddenly I’m not doing the original thing at all.\n\nIt’s like what I was saying—what brings me in is I can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. One saw starting up, somebody walking past, even just a thought about another job, and I’m pulled off. Then I look around and the whole day has gotten away from me, and half the chores are still sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're in a quiet environment, like a library or a movie theater?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Quiet places are actually kind of hard for me, even places where you’re supposed to just sit and be still.\n\nLike at home, if everything’s quiet, I’ll catch myself tapping my fingers or shifting around in the chair. At church too—I’ll whisper something to my husband when I know I shouldn’t, or I’ll get up like I need to do something, but there’s not really any good reason. It’s just… sitting still in that quiet feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nAnd then I feel bad about it, because I know I’m being distracting, but I can’t seem to settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble engaging in leisure activities or hobbies quietly, without getting distracted or feeling the need to get up and do something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, even if it’s supposed to be relaxing, I have a hard time just doing it quietly. At home, if I sit down for something, I’ll start tapping my fingers or shifting around, and then I feel like I need to get up, even if there’s no real reason.\n\nChurch is like that too. I know I’m supposed to be quiet, but I’ll catch myself whispering to my husband, or moving around in the seat, or wanting to stand up and do something. And then I’m thinking about how noisy the pew is, or whether someone noticed, and—well, then I’m not really settled at all. Sitting still in quiet just feels almost unbearable sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness happen in about the same way at work, at home, and when you're doing things you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it shows up in all of those places, but it hurts me the most when there are consequences, if that makes sense.\n\nAt work, it’s not just me being scattered—my mistakes and delays have actually cost me commissions. I’ve had to redo work on jobs because I missed something or didn’t keep track of what needed ordering. And now I can tell the crew doesn’t want to lean on me as much for scheduling or materials, which… that’s hard, because I’ve been doing this a long time.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just in a different way. My husband has had to take over the bills and reminders because I’ll misplace papers or miss what needs to be handled. I’ll put something “somewhere safe,” and then of course I can’t find it. Then I’m tearing through drawers, and that turns into reorganizing a whole cabinet, and—sorry, I know I’m wandering. But yes, it’s pretty much across the board.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're just as easily distracted and restless when you're doing something you really enjoy, like a hobby, as when you're doing everyday tasks or chores?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when it’s something I like, I still get pulled off track. It’s not really about whether I enjoy it or not.\n\nWhat brings me in is that I just can’t keep my mind on one thing anymore. Like, I’ll be trying to focus, and one saw starting up, or somebody walking past the office, or even just a thought about another job I need to check on will pull me away. Not physically always, but my head is gone. And then I’ll lose the thread of what I was doing, and next thing I know the whole day has gotten away from me.\n\nIt’s frustrating, because it’s not that I don’t care. I do care. I just get pulled off so easily.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "duration_verified": "symptoms have been present for over a year, since the patient's work changed after a wrist injury",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "onset of symptoms in adulthood, after a change in work responsibilities",
      "symptoms cause significant distress or problems in daily life"
    ]
  }
}
